:
Simulation results with pseudo-dense
\beta_{0}^{pd}
and dense
x^{(4)}
for the Toeplitz (or auto-regressive) covariance matrix
\Sigma^{\mathrm{ar}}
under the
t_{2}
distributed noise and the MAR setting (
23
).
Top Left:
Boxplots of the absolute bias.
Top Right:
Coverage probabilities with standard error bars.
Bottom Left:
Average lengths of confidence intervals with standard error bars.
Bottom Right:
Four comparative “QQ-plots” of the transformed studentized debiased estimators
\Phi\left(\sqrt{n}\cdot\widehat{\sigma}_{n}^{-1}(x)\left[\widehat{m}(x)-m_{0}(x)\right]\right)
obtained from different debiasing methods, where
\widehat{\sigma}_{n}(x)^{2}
is the estimated (asymptotic) variance of
\widehat{m}(x)
by each method and
\Phi(\cdot)
is the CDF of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

4.4
Simulation Results for the Proposed Debiasing Method With Nonparametrically Estimated Propensity Scores

As shown in
Theorem 4
and
Theorem 7
, the consistency and asymptotic normality of the proposed debiasing method do not require any parametric assumption on the propensity score
\mathrm{P}(R=1|X)
under MAR condition. Therefore, we now conduct additional simulation studies for our debiasing method in which the propensity scores
\pi(X_{i}),i=1,...,n
are estimated by the following nonlinear/nonparametric machine learning methods based on the observed data
\{(X_{i},R_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. All of these methods are implemented in the
scikit-learn
package
(
Pedregosa et al. 2011
)
in Python.

Naive Bayes (“NB”):
We adapt Gaussian naive Bayes method
(
Zhang 2004
)
to model the propensity scores through
\pi(X_{i})=\mathrm{P}(R_{i}|X_{i1},...,X_{in})=\frac{\mathrm{P}(R_{i})\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{P}(X_{ik}|R_{i})}{\mathrm{P}(X_{i1},...,X_{in})}
for any
i=1,...,n
, where
\mathrm{P}(X_{ik}|R_{i})
is the density of
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{R},\sigma_{R}^{2})
and
\mu_{R},\sigma_{R}
are estimated by the maximum likelihood approach.

Random Forest (“RF”):
We implement the random forest method
(
Breiman 2001
)
with 100 trees, bootstrapping samples, and the Gini impurity to measure the quality of a split.

Support Vector Machine (“SVM”):
We fit a support vector machine
(
Chen et al. 2005
)
with the Gaussian radial basis function to classify the missingness indicator
R_{i}
based on the observed covariate vector
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
i=1,...,n
. The outputs of the trained SVM are regarded as the surrogates of primitive estimated propensity scores.

Neural Network (“NN”):
We train a neural network model
(
Hinton 1990
)
with two hidden layers of size
80\times 50
and use the rectified linear unit function
h(x)=\max\{x,0\}
as the activation function. The learning rate
\eta_{\text{NN}}
is initially set to
0.001
as long as the training loss keeps decreasing and is adaptively changed to
\eta_{\text{NN}}/5
when two consecutive epochs fail to decrease the training loss.

For the above methods, we also consider their calibrated versions through the Platt’s logistic model
(
Platt 1999
)
that fits an extra logistic regression on the estimated propensity scores
\widehat{\pi}_{i},i=1,...,n
. However, since the errors of estimated propensity scores and the final performances of the proposed debiased estimator worsens after we apply the calibration to the estimated propensity scores, we choose not to report them here.

The covariate vectors
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},i=1,...,n
are again sampled independently from
\mathcal{N}_{d}(\bm{0},\Sigma^{\mathrm{cs}})
with
d=1000
and
n=900
, and the noise variables
\epsilon_{i},i=1,...,n
are generated independently from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
as in
Section 4.1
. To increase the complexity of estimating the propensity scores, we generate the missing indicators
R_{i},i=1,...,n
for the outcome variables
Y_{i},i=1,...,n
through a different MAR mechanism than the one in
Section 4.1
as:

\mathrm{P}(R_{i}=1|X_{i})=\Phi\left(-4+\sum_{k=1}^{K}Z_{ik}\right),

(24)

where
\Phi(\cdot)
is the CDF of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and the vector
(Z_{i1},...,Z_{iK})
contains all polynomial combinations of the first eight components
X_{i1},...,X_{i8}
of the covariate vector
X_{i}
with degrees less than or equal to two (
i.e.
, including the linear, quadratic, and one-way interaction terms).

The simulation results for our debiasing method under the oracle propensity scores (“Oracle”), the Lasso-type logistic regression (
28
) (“LR”), and the aforementioned nonparametric methods for the propensity score estimation are shown in
Figure 5
. We provide more comprehensive results with the evaluation metrics from
Section 4.2
and an additional measure for the average mean absolute error (“Avg-MAE”) for the estimated propensity scores in
Figure 9
,
Table 7
, as well as additional “QQ-plots” in
Figure 8
of
Section D.1
.

Figure 5
:
Simulation results for our debiasing method with sparse
\beta_{0}^{sp}
and (weakly) dense
x^{(4)}
when the propensity scores are estimated by various machine learning methods under the new MAR setting (
24
).
Top Left:
Boxplots of the absolute bias.
Top Right:
Coverage probabilities with standard error bars.
Middle Left:
Average lengths of confidence intervals with standard deviation bars.
Middle Right:
Letter-valued plots of the MAE of estimated propensity scores.
Bottom Two Rows:
Histograms of the studentized debiased estimators under different methods. We also order the performance metrics according to their means in the first four panels.

The main conclusion from this simulation study is that the performance of our debiasing method can be further improved when the propensity scores are better estimated. Under the MAR mechanism (
24
) and Gaussian design on covariate vectors
X_{i},i=1,...,n
, neural network and Gaussian naive Bayes models outperform other machine learning methods in estimating the propensity scores and lead to the subsequent debiased estimators with lower biases, better coverages, and shorter confidence intervals in general. Conversely, the Lasso-type logistic regression is mis-specified and consequently degrades the performance of the resulting debiased estimator. Another interesting phenomenon from these simulation results is that our debiasing method sometimes has better performances when using the estimated propensity scores
\widehat{\pi}_{i},i=1,...,n
than plugging in the oracle propensity scores
\pi_{i}=\pi(X_{i}),i=1,...,n
. Such a paradox has been analyzed in the propensity score matching
(
Rosenbaum 1987
)
, the IPW estimator for causal inference
(
Robins et al. 1992
;
Su et al. 2023
)
and other general parameter estimation problems in the presence of a nuisance parameter
(
Henmi and Eguchi 2004
;
Hitomi et al. 2008
;
Lok 2021
)
. A rigorous study of this paradox on our debiasing method is beyond the scope of this paper.

5
Real-World Application

We showcase a real application of our proposed debiasing method to the stellar mass inference on selected galaxies in the Sloan Digital Sky Survey, Fourth Phase and Data Release 16 (SDSS-IV DR16;
Ahumada et al. 2020
)
1
1

1

See
https://www.sdss4.org/dr16
.
.

5.1
Background and Study Design

SDSS-IV DR16 contains three main survey data: the Extended Baryon Oscillation Spectroscopic Survey (eBOSS;
Dawson et al. 2016
), Mapping Nearby Galaxies at APO (MaNGA;
Bundy et al. 2014
), and APO
Galactic Evolution Experiment 2 (APOGEE-2;
Majewski et al. 2017
), among which the eBOSS survey and its predecessor BOSS cover a broad range of cosmological scales and observes most galaxies in the Universe. While a couple of value added catalogs for the estimated stellar masses of observed galaxies based on the BOSS spectra are available
(
Blanton et al. 2005
;
Conroy et al. 2009
;
Chang et al. 2015
)
, we focus on the eBOSS Firefly value-added catalog
(
Comparat et al. 2017
)

2
2

2

See
https://www.sdss4.org/dr17/spectro/galaxy_firefly/
.

for our stellar mass inference study here given its timeliness and completeness. In more details, this catalog fits combinations of single-burst stellar population models to spectroscopic data that have a positive definite redshift and are classified as galaxies by SDSS-IV DR16, following an iterative best-fitting process controlled by the Bayesian Information Criterion in order to produce stellar masses and other stellar properties of the galaxies
(
Wilkinson et al. 2017
)
. There are a fraction of missing data in the estimated stellar masses by the Firefly catalog due to the limiting usage of the observational run in SDSS-IV DR16 dedicated to galaxy targets, data contamination, and misclassification of galaxies as stars
(
Comparat et al. 2017
)
. Given that we will incorporate various spectroscopic and photometric properties of the observed galaxies into the covariate vector for our stellar mass inference task, it is reasonable that the estimated stellar masses are missing at random.

Since nearly all the existing catalogs only yield point estimates to the stellar mass, we intend to leverage our debiasing inference method to answer two scientific questions:

•

Question 1:
How can we produce uncertainty measures (or confidence intervals) for the estimated stellar mass of a newly observed galaxy based on its observed spectroscopic and photometric properties?

•

Question 2:
Is it statistically significant that the stellar mass is negatively correlated with its distance to the nearby cosmic filament structures?

To this end, we fetch the spectroscopic and photometric properties of
n=1185
observed galaxies within a thin redshift slice
0.4\sim 0.4005
from SDSS-IV DR16 database as basic covariates. Such a thin redshift slice helps control the redshift distortions caused by the Kaiser effect (
i.e.
, galaxies falling towards the galaxy cluster;
Kaiser 1987
) and the “Finger-Of-God” effects (
i.e.
, the elongation of galaxy distributions along the light-of-sight direction;
Jackson 1972
). Among the galaxies in the selected redshift slice, 30.2% of their stellar masses are missing in the Firefly catalog under the “Chabrier” initial stellar mass function
(
Chabrier 2003
)
and the “MILES” stellar population model
(
Maraston and Strömbäck 2011
)
. In addition, the covariate vector for each galaxy consists of right ascension, declination, ugriz bands and their measurement errors, model and cmodel magnitudes for ugriz bandpasses, galactic extinction corrections for ugriz bandpass, median signal-to-noise per pixel within each ugriz bandpass, original and best-fit template spectra projected onto ugriz filters as well as their inverse variances, sky flux in each of the ugriz imaging filters, and signal-to-noise squared for spectrograph #1 and #2, at g=20.20, r=20.20, i=20.20 for SDSS spectrograph spectra. To capture the nonlinear patterns of stellar mass estimation and handle those extreme values, we generate additional covariates by applying the logarithmic transformations
x\mapsto\text{sign}(x)\cdot\log(1+|x|)
to ugriz bands, galactic extinction corrections, and those flux features. We also remove those covariates whose correlation coefficients are higher than 0.95 before generating univariate B-spline base covariates of polynomial order 3 with 40 knots. The reason why we adopt the B-spline base covariates instead of the usual polynomial combinations is that the covariates generated by univariate B-spline bases tends to be less linearly correlated and can facilitate the subsequent inference task. To address
Question 2
above, we compute the angular diameter distances from the galaxies to the two-dimensional spherical cosmic filaments constructed by the directional subspace constrained mean shift algorithm in
Zhang and Chen 2023
;
Zhang et al. 2022
on SDSS-IV DR16 data.
3
3

3

This cosmic web catalog can be downloaded at
https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.6244866
.
We also control the confounding effects of nearby galaxy clusters by including the angular diameter distances from galaxies to the estimated local modes and intersections of filaments as extra covariates. The final dimension of the covariate vector for each galaxy in the selected redshift slice is
d=1409
, and we take the usual logarithm of the Firefly stellar mass of each galaxy as the outcome variable.

5.2
Results

Figure 6
:
Stellar mass inference with our debiasing and related methods.
Left Panel:
Estimated propensity scores by “LR”, “NN”, and “NNcal”.
Middle Panel:
95% confidence intervals by different methods for the estimated stellar mass of the new galaxy, addressing
Question 1
.
Right Panel:
95% confidence intervals by different methods for the estimated regression coefficient associated with the distance to cosmic filaments, addressing
Question 2
.

When approaching
Question 1
, we randomly select a galaxy in the nearby redshift slice
0.4005\sim 0.401
as a new observation and construct its associated covariate vector as the query point
x^{(Q1)}
following the procedures in
Section 5.1
. As for
Question 2
, we set the query point as
x^{(Q2)}=(0,0,1,0,...,0)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where the only nonzero entry corresponds to the covariate of the angular diameter distance to cosmic filaments. We estimate the propensity scores (
i.e.
, the probabilities of having a non-missing stellar masses) on the galaxies in the redshift slice by the Lasso-type logistic regression (“LR”) in
Section C
and the neural network (“NN”) model described in
Section 4.4
. Nevertheless, as shown by the left panel of
Figure 6
that the estimated propensity scores by the neural network model are too extreme,
i.e.
, close to 0 or 1, we also consider calibrating them through the Platt’s logistic model
(
Platt 1999
)
, which is denoted by “NNcal”.

Figure 6
displays the inference results by our proposed debiasing method and the classical approaches in
Section 4.1
applied to the complete-case data. Due to the sparsity structure of the design matrix created by B-spline bases, “DL-Jav” and “DL-vdG” fail to produce their inference results. For
Question 1
, the 95% confidence intervals obtained from our debiasing method cover the stellar mass of the new galaxy in the Firefly catalog no matter how we estimate the propensity scores or select the tuning parameter, while the Lasso refitting method underestimates the stellar mass in a statistically significant level. For
Question 2
, the 95% confidence intervals produced by our debiasing and Lasso refitting methods for the regression coefficient of the distance to cosmic filaments are both below 0, aligning with the existing findings that galaxies are less massive when they are farther away from the cosmic filaments
(
Alpaslan et al. 2016
;
Kraljic et al. 2018
;
Malavasi et al. 2022
)
. On the contrary, the 95% confidence interval obtained from the ridge projection method contains zero and cannot draw a statistically significant conclusion about the relation between stellar masses of galaxies and their distances to cosmic filaments. These results consolidate the usefulness of our proposed debiasing method in practice.

6
Discussion

This paper proposes a novel debiasing method for conducting valid inference on high-dimensional linear models with MAR outcomes. We establish the asymptotic normality of the debiasing method and illuminate its statistical and computational efficiencies through the dual formulation of the key debiasing program. Simulation studies and real-world applications of our proposed method demonstrate its finite-sample superiority over the existing high-dimensional inference methods in the literature. There are several potential applications and extensions that can further advance the impacts of our debiasing method.

1. Applications in causal inference:
As discussed by
Ding and Li 2018
;
Chakrabortty et al. 2019
, the missing outcome setting in this paper incorporates the classical setup in causal inference under the framework of potential outcome
(
Rubin 1974
)
as a special case. Specifically, the observable data in causal inference problems are of the form
\left(\mathbb{Y},T,X\right)\in\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
T\in\{0,1\}
denotes a binary treatment assignment indicator and
\mathbb{Y}=T\cdot Y(1)+(1-T)\cdot Y(0)
with
Y(0),Y(1)
being the potential outcomes of
Y
within the control group
T=0
and the treatment group
T=1
, respectively. Given that at most one of the potential outcomes
Y(0),Y(1)
for each subject is observed, the potential outcome framework can be related to our problem setting by taking
(Y,R)=(Y(1),T)
in the treatment group or
(Y,R)=(Y(0),1-T)
in the control group. Therefore, our debiasing inference method can be applied to high-dimensional causal inference problems.

First, our debiasing method can be used to conduct valid statistical inference on the regression function (or the conditional mean outcome) of the treatment group if we focus on the observational data
\{(Y_{i},R_{i},X_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}=\{(Y(1)_{i},T_{i},X_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
or the control group if the data of interest is
\{(Y_{i},R_{i},X_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}=\{(Y(0)_{i},1-T_{i},X_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Similar to the regression adjustment in causal inference
(
Freedman 2008
;
Lin 2013
;
Negi and Wooldridge 2021
)
, we utilize all the covariate vectors
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},i=1,...,n
in both treatment and control groups to address the above inference problem, which could provide extra efficiency gains.

Second, under Assumption
1
, our proposed debiasing program (
4
) can also be extended to conduct statistical inference on the linear average conditional treatment effect (ACTE)
\mathrm{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)|X]
with no unmeasured confounding. To this end, borrowing an idea from
Giessing and Wang 2023
, the debiasing program (
4
) can be modified as follows:

\displaystyle\argmin_{\bm{w}_{(0)},\bm{w}_{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\widehat{\pi}_{i}w_{i(1)}^{2}+(1-\widehat{\pi}_{i})w_{i(0)}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\text{ subject to }\left|\left|x-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i(1)}\cdot\widehat{\pi}_{i}\cdot X_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}}{n}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left|\left|x-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i(0)}\left(1-\widehat{\pi}_{i}\right)X_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}}{n},

where
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}>0
are the tuning parameters and
\widehat{\pi}_{i},i=1,...,n
are the estimated treatment assignment probabilities (
i.e.
, propensity scores). The debiased estimator (
5
) will become

\displaystyle\widehat{m}^{\text{debias}}(x;\widehat{\bm{w}}_{(1)},\widehat{\bm{w}}_{(0)})

\displaystyle=x^{T}\left(\widehat{\beta}_{(1)}-\widehat{\beta}_{(0)}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\widehat{w}_{i(1)}\cdot T_{i}\left(\mathbb{Y}_{i}-X_{i}^{T}\widehat{\beta}_{(1)}\right)-\widehat{w}_{i(0)}\cdot\left(1-T_{i}\right)\left(\mathbb{Y}_{i}-X_{i}^{T}\widehat{\beta}_{(0)}\right)\right].

However, this new estimator may no longer embrace the asymptotically semi-parametric efficiency, and we will leave the thorough theoretical investigation to the future work. Other related works for inferring the linear CATE with high-dimensional covariates can be found in
Chernozhukov and Semenova 2018
.

2. Model mis-specification and robustness:
Theoretical guarantees of our debiasing method are validated under the linear model (
1
) and sub-Gaussianity assumptions. While we have conducted additional simulations in
Section 4.3
to certify the robustness of our debiasing method against the violations of the sub-Gaussian noise assumption, it is of research interest to investigate the performances and theoretical implications of our debiasing method when the assumed linear model is misspecified. Given that our analysis of the inference with random design is unconditional on the covariate vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the modification technique in
Bühlmann and van de Geer 2015
might provide some insights.

3. Missing covariates:
Extending our debiasing method to address issues related to incomplete covariates remains an open problem. Notably, the problem is more challenging under high-dimensional settings because there are numerous covariates, resulting in a large number of potential missing patterns (up to
2^{d}
at most).
While there have been some efforts to leverage the MCAR assumption
(
Wang et al. 2019
)
to tackle this problem, it is noteworthy that MCAR is a strong assumption that may not hold in many real-world scenarios.
A possible remedy is to use the multiple imputation method
(
Carpenter et al. 2023
)
with a well-designed debiasing program similar to
Xue et al. 2021
, but it is unclear how to design a reliable imputation model that is compatible with the downstream debiasing program.

,
and

Contents

The supplementary material contains auxiliary technical results, additional simulation studies, and proofs. We organize its content as follows.

•

Section A
Implementation of the Proposed Debiasing Method
We explain the implementation steps of our debiasing method in detail.

•

Section B
Sufficient Conditions for Assumption
6
We provide sufficient conditions and motivating examples under which Assumption
6
holds.

•

Section C
Generalized Linear Model with Lasso Regularization for Propensity Score Estimation
We showcase an example of estimating the propensity scores via the Lasso-type generalized linear regression and derive an explicit formula for the rate of consistency
r_{\pi}
in Assumption
5
.

•

Section D
Additional Simulation Results
We furnish extensive simulation results that are not covered by
Section 4
in the main paper.

•

Section E
Proofs
We provide proofs for all the theorems and technical results in the main paper.

Appendix A
Implementation of the Proposed Debiasing Method

Our proposed debiasing method consists of three main estimation steps: (i) computing the Lasso pilot estimate (
3
), (ii) estimating the propensity scores
\pi_{i}=\pi(X_{i})=\mathrm{P}(R_{i}=1|X_{i}),i=1,...,n
, and (iii) solving the debiasing programs (
4
) and (
8
). We explain the implementation details and strategies of selecting two tuning parameters
\lambda>0
in (
3
) and
\gamma>0
in (
4
) as follows.

1. Lasso pilot estimate:
We use the complete-case (or equivalently, observed) data
(X_{i},Y_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
with
R_{i}=1
for
i=1,...,n
to construct the Lasso pilot estimate
\widehat{\beta}
. In order to select the regularization parameter
\lambda>0
in a data adaptive way, we adopt the scaled Lasso
(
Sun and Zhang 2012
)
with its universal regularization parameter
\lambda_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{2\log d}{n}}
as the initialization. Specifically, it provides an iterative algorithm to obtain the Lasso estimate
\widehat{\beta}
and a consistent estimator
\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}
of the noise level
\sigma_{\epsilon}
from the following jointly convex optimization problem:

\left(\widehat{\beta}(\widetilde{\lambda}),\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{\lambda})\right)=\argmin_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\sigma_{\epsilon}>0}\left[\frac{1}{2n\sigma_{\epsilon}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\left(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{T}\beta\right)^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{\epsilon}}{2}+\widetilde{\lambda}\left|\left|\beta\right|\right|_{1}\right],

where the regularization parameter
\widetilde{\lambda}>0
is updated iteratively as well. We also utilize this estimated noise level
\widehat{\sigma}_{\epsilon}
to construct our asymptotic
1-\tau
confidence interval (
6
).

2. Propensity score estimation:
When comparing our debiasing method with other existing high-dimensional inference methods, we utilize the Lasso-type logistic regression (
28
) in
Section C
to estimate the propensity scores
\pi_{i}=\mathrm{P}(R_{i}=1|X_{i}),i=1,...,n
, in which the regularization parameter
\zeta
is selected on 40 logarithmically spacing values within the interval
\left[0.1\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}},300\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}}\right]
via 5-fold cross-validations. However, as demonstrated in
Section 4.4
through simulation studies, our debiasing method can perform even better when the propensity scores are estimated more accurately by other nonparametric methods, reflecting the theoretical results in
Section 3
.

3. Solving the debiasing program:

When our primal debiasing program is strictly feasible, strong duality holds by Slater’s condition (Theorem 3.2.6 in
Renegar 2001
), and the debiasing weights
\widehat{w}_{i}(x),i=1,...,n
can be estimated from either the primal program (
4
) or the dual program (
8
) by the identity (
9
) in Proposition
1
. We implement our debiasing method in both Python and R. In particular, we solve the primal program (
4
) by the Python package “
CVXPY
”
(
Diamond and Boyd 2016
;
Agrawal et al. 2018
)
or the R package “
CVXR
”
(
Fu et al. 2020
)
. For the dual program (
8
), we formulate and implement the classical coordinate descent algorithm
(
Wright 2015
)
as:

\left[\widehat{\ell}(x)\right]_{j}\leftarrow\frac{\mathcal{S}_{\frac{\gamma}{n}}\left(-\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\pi}_{i}\left(\sum_{k\neq j}X_{ik}X_{jk}\left[\widehat{\ell}(x)\right]_{k}\right)-x_{j}\right)}{\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\pi}_{i}X_{ij}^{2}}\quad\text{ for }\quad j=1,...,d,

(25)

where
\mathcal{S}_{\frac{\gamma}{n}}(u)=\mathrm{sign}(u)\cdot\left(u-\frac{\gamma}{n}\right)_{+}
is the soft-thresholding operator, and the above procedure will iterate cyclically from
j=1
to
d
until convergence.

The tuning parameter
\gamma>0
of our debiasing program is selected from 41 equally spacing values between 0 and
n\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{\infty}
via 5-fold cross-validations according to the dual problem (
8
), in which
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the current query point. Here, the upper bound for the candidate values of
\gamma>0
is set to
n\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{\infty}
, because the optimal solution
\widehat{w}(x)
for the primal problem (
4
) becomes 0 when
\frac{\gamma}{n}\geq\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{\infty}
. There are three different criteria for selecting the final value of
\gamma>0
from the cross-validation results.

(i)

The first criterion selects the value of
\gamma>0
that minimizes the average cross-validated risk of the dual debiasing program (
8
), which is known as the “min-CV” rule.

(ii)

The second criterion picks the value of
\gamma>0
whose associated average cross-validated risk is at least one standard error (calculated across the 5-folded data) away from the smallest cross-validated risk and is also smaller than the one corresponding to the smallest average cross-validated risk; see Section 3.4.3 in
Breiman et al. 1984
and
Chen and Yang 2021
for details. We name this criterion as the “1SE” rule. The rationale behind the “1SE” rule is to improve the inference performance of our debiasing method by sacrificing some statistical efficiency (
i.e.
, potentially yielding a larger value of the estimated asymptotic variance) for a less biased estimator.

(iii)

The third criterion chooses the minimal value of
\gamma>0
in which the primal program is feasible for all 5 folds of the data. We call it the “min-feas” rule, which would lead to the most conservative confidence interval for our debiasing method.

We recommend the final value of
\gamma
to be chosen as any value between the “min-feas” and “1SE” rules, depending how conservative that we need for the subsequent inference from our proposed debiasing program; recall from
Section 4.2
for our simulation results.

Appendix B
Sufficient Conditions for Assumption
6

We present several sufficient conditions in Lemma
B.1
under which Assumption
6
on the sparse approximation to the population dual solution
\ell_{0}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in Definition
1
holds and illuminate these sufficient conditions through some motivating examples.

Lemma B.1

(Sufficient conditions for the sparse approximation to
\ell_{0}(x)
)
.

Under Assumption
4
, we let
\left[\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})\right]^{-1}:=M=(\bm{m}_{1},...,\bm{m}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, where
\bm{m}_{k},k=1,...,d
are columns of the matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. We also denote by
M_{S,T}\in\mathbb{R}^{|S|\times|T|}
the submatrix of
M
indexed by
S,T\subset\{1,...,d\}
and let
\sigma_{\min}(M_{S,T})
be its smallest singular value.

(a)

If each column of
M
has at most
m\geq 1
nonzero entries and the query vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
has at most
s_{x}\geq 1
nonzero entries, then Assumption
6
holds with
s_{\ell}(x)=m\cdot s_{x}
and
r_{\ell}=0
for all
n\geq 1
.

(b)

Suppose that each column
\bm{m}_{k}
of
M
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{1}(J_{k},\vartheta)
for some
J_{k}\subset\{1,...,d\}
and
\vartheta\in[0,\infty)
. If the query vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
has its support set as
\mathrm{support}(x)=S_{x}
of size at most
s_{x}>0
and an index set
J\subset\{1,...,d\}
satisfies
\sigma_{\min}(M_{J,S_{x}})>0
, then Assumption
6
holds with
s_{\ell}(x)=|J|\cdot R_{\ell}(n)
and
r_{\ell}=O\left(\frac{(1+\vartheta)\cdot K_{J,x}}{\sqrt{R_{\ell}(n)}}\right)
for some
R_{\ell}(n)\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, where
K_{J,x}=\max_{k\in S_{x}}\frac{\sqrt{s_{x}}\left|\left|M_{J,k}\right|\right|_{1}}{\sigma_{\min}(M_{J,S_{x}})}
.

(c)

Suppose that each column
\bm{m}_{k}
of
M
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{1}(J_{k},\vartheta)
for some
J_{k}\subset\{1,...,d\}
and
\vartheta\in[0,\infty)
. If the query vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{1}(J,\vartheta_{x})
with the index set
J\subset\{1,...,d\}
satisfying that
\kappa_{\min}(J,\vartheta_{x})>0
, then Assumption
6
holds with
s_{\ell}(x)=|J|\cdot R_{\ell}(n)
and
r_{\ell}=O\left(\frac{(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}}{\sqrt{R_{\ell}(n)}}\right)
for some
R_{\ell}(n)\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, where
K_{J,x}=(1+\vartheta_{x})\max\limits_{1\leq k\leq d}\frac{\sqrt{|J|}\cdot\left|\left|M_{J,k}\right|\right|_{1}}{\kappa_{\min}(J,\vartheta_{x})}
with
\kappa_{\min}(J,\vartheta_{x})=\inf\limits_{u\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(J,\vartheta_{x})}\frac{\sqrt{|J|}\cdot\left|\left|M_{J}u\right|\right|_{2}}{\left|\left|u_{J}\right|\right|_{1}}
being the compatibility constant of the submatrix
M_{J}
.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

The proof of Lemma
B.1
are inspired and modified from Lemma 4 in
Giessing and Wang 2023
.

(a) Let
S_{x}=\mathrm{support}(x)\subset\{1,...,d\}
for the fixed query vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Given that
\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left|\left|M_{k}\right|\right|_{0}\leq m
and
\ell_{0}(x)=-2\left[\mathrm{E}\left(RXX^{T}\right)\right]^{-1}x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we compute that

\displaystyle\left|\left|\ell_{0}(x)\right|\right|_{0}=\left|\left|Mx\right|\right|_{0}=\left|\left|\sum_{k=1}^{d}\bm{m}_{k}x_{k}\right|\right|_{0}=\left|\left|\sum_{k\in S_{x}}\bm{m}_{k}x_{k}\right|\right|_{0}\leq\sum_{k\in S_{x}}\left|\left|\bm{m}_{k}\right|\right|_{0}\leq m\cdot s_{x}.

Thus,
\ell_{0}(x)
by itself is sparse and we can take
\widetilde{\ell}(x)=\ell_{0}(x)
in Assumption
6
with
s_{\ell}(x)=m\cdot s_{x}
and
r_{\ell}=0
for all
n\geq 1
.

(b) Since each column of
M
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{1}(J_{k},\vartheta)
for some
J_{k}\subset\{1,...,d\}
with
|J_{k}|=|J|
and
\vartheta\in[0,\infty)
, we know that
\left|\left|M_{S_{x}^{c},j}\right|\right|_{1}\leq(1+\vartheta)\left|\left|M_{S_{x},j}\right|\right|_{1}
for all
j\in S_{x}
. Moreover,
\sigma_{\min}(M_{J,S_{x}})=\inf_{\mathrm{support}(u)=S_{x}}\frac{\left|\left|M_{J}u\right|\right|_{2}}{\left|\left|u\right|\right|_{2}}>0
by our assumption. Now, we compute that

\displaystyle\left|\left|\left[\ell_{0}(x)\right]_{J^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle=2\left|\left|\left[Mx\right]_{J^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle=2\left|\left|\sum_{k=1}^{d}M_{J^{c},k}\cdot x_{k}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{k\in S_{x}}\left|\left|M_{J^{c},k}\right|\right|_{1}|x_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{k\in S_{x}}\left(\left|\left|M_{J^{c}\cap J_{k}^{c},k}\right|\right|_{1}+\left|\left|M_{J^{c}\cap J_{k},k}\right|\right|_{1}\right)|x_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{k\in S_{x}}\left(\left|\left|M_{J_{k}^{c},k}\right|\right|_{1}+\left|\left|M_{J_{k},k}\right|\right|_{1}\right)|x_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\vartheta)\sum_{k\in S_{x}}\left|\left|M_{J_{k},k}\right|\right|_{1}|x_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\vartheta)\sqrt{s_{x}}\cdot\max_{k\in S_{x}}\left|\left|M_{J_{k},k}\right|\right|_{1}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\vartheta)\sqrt{s_{x}}\cdot\max_{k\in S_{x}}\left|\left|M_{J_{k},k}\right|\right|_{1}\left|\left|(Mx)_{J}\right|\right|_{2}\sup_{\mathrm{support}(u)=S_{x}}\frac{\left|\left|u\right|\right|_{2}}{\left|\left|(Mu)_{J}\right|\right|_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2(1+\vartheta)\sqrt{s_{x}}\max_{k\in S_{x}}\left|\left|M_{J,k}\right|\right|_{1}}{\sigma_{\min}(M_{J,S_{x}})}\cdot\left|\left|(Mx)_{J}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}\left|\left|\left[\ell_{0}(x)\right]_{S_{x}}\right|\right|_{1},

showing that
\ell_{0}(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(J,(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}\right)
. By Theorem 2.5 in
Foucart and Rauhut 2013
, there exists a
s_{\ell}(x)
-sparse approximation
\widetilde{\ell}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the population dual solution
\ell_{0}(x)
such that

\left|\left|\widetilde{\ell}(x)-\ell_{0}(x)\right|\right|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{s_{\ell}(x)}}\left|\left|\ell_{0}(x)\right|\right|_{1}.

Combining with
\ell_{0}(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(J,(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}\right)
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widetilde{\ell}(x)-\ell_{0}(x)\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{s_{\ell}(x)}}\left[\left|\left|\left[\ell_{0}(x)\right]_{J}\right|\right|_{1}+\left|\left|\left[\ell_{0}(x)\right]_{J^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}}{2\sqrt{s_{\ell}(x)}}\cdot\left|\left|\left[\ell_{0}(x)\right]_{J}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}}{2}\sqrt{\frac{|J|}{s_{\ell}(x)}}\cdot\left|\left|\ell_{0}(x)\right|\right|_{2}.

Thus, when
s_{\ell}(x)=|J|\cdot R_{\ell}(n)
, it follows that

\left|\left|\widetilde{\ell}(x)-\ell_{0}(x)\right|\right|_{2}\leq\frac{1+(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}}{2\sqrt{R_{\ell}(n)}}\cdot\left|\left|\ell_{0}(x)\right|\right|_{2},

so we take
r_{\ell}=O\left(\frac{(1+\vartheta)K(J,x)}{\sqrt{R_{\ell}(n)}}\right)
to fulfill Assumption
6
.

(c) Since
x\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(J,\vartheta_{x})
, it follows that
\left|\left|x_{J^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\leq\vartheta_{x}\left|\left|x_{J}\right|\right|_{1}
. Now, we compute that

\displaystyle\left|\left|\left[\ell_{0}(x)\right]_{J^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle=2\left|\left|\left(Mx\right)_{J^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle=2\left|\left|\sum_{k=1}^{d}M_{J^{c},k}x_{k}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left|\left|M_{J^{c},k}\right|\right|_{1}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\vartheta_{x})\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left|\left|M_{J^{c},k}\right|\right|_{1}\left|\left|x_{J}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\vartheta_{x})\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left|\left|M_{J^{c},k}\right|\right|_{1}\left|\left|(Mx)_{J}\right|\right|_{2}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(J,a_{0})}\frac{\left|\left|u_{J}\right|\right|_{1}}{\left|\left|(Mu)_{J}\right|\right|_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\vartheta)(1+\vartheta_{x})\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left|\left|M_{J_{k},k}\right|\right|_{1}\left|\left|(Mx)_{J}\right|\right|_{2}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(J,\vartheta_{x})}\frac{\left|\left|u_{J}\right|\right|_{1}}{\left|\left|M_{J}u\right|\right|_{2}}

\displaystyle=2(1+\vartheta)(1+\vartheta_{x})\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\frac{\sqrt{|J|}\cdot\left|\left|M_{J_{k},k}\right|\right|_{1}}{\kappa_{\min}(J,\vartheta_{x})}\cdot\left|\left|\left(Mx\right)_{J}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}\left|\left|\left[\ell_{0}(x)\right]_{J}\right|\right|_{1},

showing that
\ell_{0}(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(J,(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}\right)
. Therefore, as in the proof of Part (b), we conclude that Assumption
6
holds with
s_{\ell}(x)=|J|\cdot R_{\ell}(n)
and
r_{\ell}=O\left(\frac{(1+\vartheta)K_{J,x}}{\sqrt{R_{\ell}(n)}}\right)
.
∎

Remark B.1
.

The constants
\kappa_{\min}(M_{S_{x},S_{x}})
and
\kappa_{\min}(J,\vartheta_{x})
in (b) and (c) of Lemma
B.1
are well-known in the statistical literature.
Sun and Zhang 2012
;
Sun and Zhang 2013
introduced the eigenvalue version of
\sigma_{\min}(M_{J,S_{x}})>0
to study the least-square and precision matrix estimations after the scaled lasso selection. The compatibility constant
\kappa_{\min}(J,\vartheta_{x})
appears even more commonly in the literature (see Section 6.2.2 in
Bühlmann and van De Geer 2011
), given that it is also related to our restricted eigenvalue condition (
17
) as:

\kappa_{\min}(J,a_{0})=\inf\limits_{u\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(J,\vartheta_{x})}\frac{\sqrt{|J|}\cdot\left|\left|M_{J}u\right|\right|_{2}}{\left|\left|u_{J}\right|\right|_{1}}\geq\inf\limits_{u\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(J,\vartheta_{x})}\frac{\left|\left|M_{J}u\right|\right|_{2}}{\left|\left|u\right|\right|_{2}}.

Now, we illustrate these sufficient conditions in Lemma
B.1
through the linear regression model (
1
) under both MCAR and MAR data settings and connect the sparsity structure of
\left[\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})\right]^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with the well-studied covariance matrix
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
of the covariate vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the statistical literature.

Example 1

(Linear regression model under the MCAR mechanism)
.

Recall the high-dimensional sparse linear regression model (
1
) in Assumption
1
as:

Y=X^{T}\beta+\epsilon\quad\text{ with }\quad\mathrm{E}(\epsilon|X)=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{E}\left(\epsilon^{2}|X\right)=\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}.

Furthermore, we suppose, without loss of generality, that the covariate vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is centered in this example,
i.e.
,
\mathrm{E}(X)=0
. In order to study the sparsity structure of

\ell_{0}(x)=-2\left[\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})\right]^{-1}x:=-2Mx,

it suffices to exploit the (weak) sparsity of
M=\left[\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})\right]^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
by Lemma
B.1
when the query point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is sparse or lie in a cone of dominant coordinates
\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(J,\vartheta_{x}\right)
. In particular, under the MCAR setting, we know that
R{\perp\!\!\!\perp}(Y,X)
, and the propensity score satisfies
\pi(X,Y)=\mathrm{P}(R=1|X,Y)=\mathrm{P}(R=1)
so that

M=\left[\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})\right]^{-1}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{P}(R=1)}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}

will be sparse if and only if the precision matrix
\Sigma^{-1}
is sparse. Examples of sparse precision matrices include the auto-regressive model of order
q\geq 1
(whose precision matrix is banded; see,
e.g.
, Section 9.2 in
Lindsey 2004
) and solutions to the graphical Lasso model
(
Friedman et al. 2008
)
or other
L_{1}
-minimization programs
(
Cai et al. 2016
)
. In addition, as the sufficient conditions in Lemma
B.1
(b,c) only require each column of
\Sigma^{-1}
to lie in
\mathcal{C}_{1}(J,\vartheta)
for some
J\subset\{1,...,d\}
, any moving-average process of order
q\geq 1
(whose precision matrix is Toeplitz; recall
\Sigma^{\mathrm{ar}}
in
Section 4.1
) and other short-range dependent processes
(
Rosenblatt 2015
)
will have their precision matrices fulfilling the requirements as well.

Example 2

(Linear regression model under the MAR mechanism)
.

Under the same linear model setup in Example
1
, we consider the MAR setting
R{\perp\!\!\!\perp}Y|X
, in which the propensity score becomes
\pi(X)=\mathrm{P}(R=1|X)
. We consider several scenarios that relate the sparsity structures of
M=\left[\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})\right]^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
to the covariance matrix
\Sigma=\mathrm{E}(XX^{T})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

1.

Suppose that the propensity score
\pi(X)
depends on at most
1\leq s_{\pi}\ll d
covariates in
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
i.e.
, without loss of generality,

\pi(x)=\mathrm{P}\left(R=1|X=(x_{1},...,x_{d})^{T}\right)=\pi(x_{1},...,x_{s_{\pi}}).

(26)

We further assume that the first few covariates
X^{(1)},...,X^{(d_{1})}
with
d_{1}\geq s_{\pi}
are independent of the rest covariates
X^{(d_{1}+1)},...,X^{(d_{1}+d_{2})}
in the covariate vector
X=\left(X^{(1)},...,X^{(d)}\right)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d=d_{1}+d_{2}
. Then, the covariance matrix
\Sigma
is block diagonal as
\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{1}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\Sigma_{2}\end{pmatrix}
, where
\Sigma_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}}
and
\Sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{2}}
. Under the sparsity assumption (
26
) on the propensity score
\pi(X)
, we know that

\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})=\mathrm{E}\left(\pi(X)XX^{T}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{1}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\Sigma_{2}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{ and }\quad M=\left[\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})\right]^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, the sufficient condition (a) in Lemma
B.1
holds.

2.

More generally, the complete-case gram matrix
\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}&\widetilde{\Sigma}_{12}\\
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}&\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}\end{pmatrix}
has its inverse as:

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}+\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{12}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}&-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{12}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}\right)^{-1}\\
-\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}&\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}\right)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

If the entries of
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}=\widetilde{\Sigma}_{12}^{T}
are relatively small in their absolute values, which happens when,
e.g.
,
\Sigma=\mathrm{E}(XX^{T})
is diagonally dominant (such as banded, Toeplitz, or other sparse covariance matrices; recall
\Sigma^{\mathrm{cs}}
and
\Sigma^{\mathrm{ar}}
in
Section 4.1
) together with (
26
), then the off-diagonal block matrices of
M=\left[\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})\right]^{-1}
given by

-\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}=-\left[\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{12}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{21}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{22}\right)^{-1}\right]^{T}

are also small in their absolute values compared with the diagonal block matrices of
M
and therefore, the sufficient conditions (b) and (c) in Lemma
B.1
apply.

Appendix C
Generalized Linear Model with Lasso Regularization for Propensity Score Estimation

Recall that the consistency and asymptotic normality of our proposed debiasing method holds as long as the propensity scores are consistently estimated on the sample covariate vectors
X_{i},i=1,...,n
under a mild rate of convergence (
21
). While it is not necessary to estimate the propensity scores via parametric models for our debiasing method (see
Section 4.4
for our simulation results), the generalized linear model always serves as a baseline approach and can approximate many complex nonparametric models by including as many (well-designed) covariates as possible. As a result, the covariate vector is often high-dimensional with
d\gg n
in practice, and we consider estimating the propensity score
\pi(x)=\mathrm{P}(R=1|X=x)
under the MAR assumption (Assumption
1
) with the data
\{(X_{i},R_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{0,1\}
by the Lasso-type generalized linear regression defined as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\theta}&=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\log\left[\frac{\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta)}{1-\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta)}\right]-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\left[1-\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\right]+\zeta\left|\left|\theta\right|\right|_{1}\right\}\\
&:=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}(\theta)+\zeta\left|\left|\theta\right|\right|_{1}\right],\end{split}

(27)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is the negative log-likelihood function associated with a known link function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to(0,1)
and
\zeta>0
is a regularization parameter. In particular, when the logistic link function
\varphi_{\mathrm{log}}(u)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-u}}
is applied, the above regression problem (
27
) reduces to the Lasso-type logistic regression as:

\widehat{\theta}=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[R_{i}X_{i}^{T}\theta-\log\left(1+e^{X_{i}^{T}\theta}\right)\right]+\zeta\left|\left|\theta\right|\right|_{1}\right\}.

(28)

With the estimate
\widehat{\theta}
from the Lasso-type generalized linear regression (
27
), it is also feasible to derive an explicit formula of
r_{\pi}=r_{\pi}(n,\delta)
in Assumption
5
for the estimated propensity scores
\widehat{\pi}_{i},i=1,...,n
under the following regularity conditions.

Assumption 7

(Sparse generalized linear propensity score model)
.

Under Assumption
1
, the missingness variable
R\in\{0,1\}
is generated from the following model as:

R|X\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}\left(\varphi(X^{T}\theta_{0})\right),

(29)

where
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to(0,1)
is a known link function and
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a high-dimensional sparse vector with support
S_{\theta}
and
|S_{\theta}|=\left|\left|\theta_{0}\right|\right|_{0}=s_{\theta}\ll d
.

Assumption 8

(
\mathcal{C}_{1}(S_{\theta},3)
-restricted eigenvalue condition on
\mathrm{E}(RXX^{T})
)
.

The
\mathcal{C}_{1}(S_{\theta},3)
-restricted minimum eigenvalue of the complete-case population gram matrix
\mathrm{E}\left(RXX^{T}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is bounded away from zero,
i.e.
, there exists an absolute constant
\rho>0
such that

\mathrm{E}\left[R(X^{T}v)^{2}\right]>\rho\left|\left|v\right|\right|_{2}^{2}\quad\text{ for any }\quad v\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(S_{\theta},3),

where
S_{\theta}
is the support of the
s_{\theta}
-sparse vector
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Assumption 9

(Regular link function)
.

The link function
\varphi
satisfies the following regularity conditions.

(a)

\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
is twice differentiable, nondecreasing, and
L_{\varphi}
-Lipschitz on
\mathbb{R}
as well as concave on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
for some absolute constant
L_{\varphi}>0
; also, for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
, it holds that
\varphi(x)+\varphi(-x)=1
.

(b)

There exists an absolute constant
A_{\varphi}>0
such that
\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(u)}{1-\varphi(u)}\leq A_{\varphi}
for all
u\geq 0
.

(c)

For
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}(\theta)
defined in (
27
), there exists an absolute constant
A_{H}>1
such that the Hessian matrix
\nabla\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}(\theta)
can be expressed as
\nabla\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}(\theta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\theta;R_{i},X_{i})X_{i}X_{i}^{T}

for some
H(\theta;R_{i},X_{i})>0
satisfying

\left|\log H(\theta+b;R_{i},X_{i})-\log H(\theta;R_{i},X_{i})\right|\leq A_{H}\left(|X_{i}^{T}\theta|^{2}+|X_{i}^{T}b|^{2}+|X_{i}^{T}b|\right)

for any
\theta,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
i=1,...,n
.

The sparse generalized linear regression setup in Assumption
7
is commonly imposed in the statistical literature to handle high-dimensional data
(
van de Geer 2008
;
Huang and Zhang 2012
;
Cai et al. 2021
)
. Assumption
8
is the restricted eigenvalue condition over the cone
\mathcal{C}_{1}(S_{\theta},3)
; recall (
17
). In Assumption
9
, the constants
A_{\varphi},A_{H}>0
only depend on the link function
\varphi
. A similar condition is also imposed in
Cai et al. 2021
to establish the consistency of
\widehat{\theta}
in (
27
) and derive the asymptotic normality of their debiased estimator for
\widehat{\theta}
. One can verify by direct calculations and Example 8 in
Huang and Zhang 2012
that the logistic link function
\varphi_{\mathrm{log}}(u)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-u}}
satisfies Assumption
9
. Furthermore, Section 3 of the supplement in
Cai et al. 2021
also verifies Assumption
9
for the probit link function
\varphi_{\mathrm{probit}}(u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{u}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}dt
, the class of generalized logistic functions, as well as the CDFs of Student’s
t_{\nu}
-distributions with any
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
in their latent variable model (Example 3 in their supplement).

Remark C.1
.

Assumption
9
(b) implies that
\max\left\{\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(u)}{\varphi(u)},\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(u)}{1-\varphi(u)}\right\}\leq A_{\varphi}

for the same constant
A_{\varphi}>0
. This is because if
u\geq 0
, then by Assumption
9
(a),
\varphi(u)\geq\frac{1}{2}
and consequently,

\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(u)}{\varphi(u)}\leq\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(u)}{1-\varphi(u)}\leq A_{\varphi}.

On the other hand, if
u<0
, then Assumption
9
(a) also shows that
\varphi(u)\leq\frac{1}{2}
and therefore,

\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(u)}{1-\varphi(u)}\leq\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(u)}{\varphi(u)}=\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(-u)}{1-\varphi(-u)}\leq A_{\varphi},

where we use the condition that
\varphi(x)+\varphi(-x)=1
and
\varphi^{\prime}(x)-\varphi^{\prime}(-x)=0
in Assumption
9
(a).

As for Assumption
9
(c), some algebra show that

H(\theta;R_{i},X_{i})=-\frac{R_{i}\left[\varphi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta)-\left(\varphi^{\prime}(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\right)^{2}\right]}{\left[\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\right]^{2}}+\frac{(1-R_{i})\left[\varphi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\left(1-\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\right)+\left(\varphi^{\prime}(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\right)^{2}\right]}{\left[1-\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\right]^{2}}.

Hence, a sufficient condition for
H(\theta;R_{i},X_{i})>0
is that

\left[\varphi^{\prime}(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\right]^{2}>\max\Big\{\varphi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta),\varphi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\left[\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta)-1\right]\Big\},

(30)

which is also equivalent to
\left[\varphi^{\prime}(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\right]^{2}>\varphi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{T}\theta)\varphi(X_{i}^{T}\theta)
for all
X_{i}^{T}\theta<0
. This condition can be easily verified for the logistic link function
\varphi_{\mathrm{log}}(u)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-u}}
and the probit link function
\varphi_{\mathrm{probit}}(u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{u}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}dt
.

Under the above regularity conditions, we derive the explicit formula for
r_{\pi}=r_{\pi}(n,\delta)
as follows.

Proposition C.1
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4}>0
be some absolute constants. Suppose that Assumptions
2
,
7
,
8
, and
9
hold as well as

d\geq s_{\theta}+2,\quad A_{1}\phi_{s_{\theta}}\log\left(\frac{\phi_{s_{\theta}}}{\rho}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\phi_{1}s_{\theta}\log d}{n}}\leq\frac{\rho}{8},\;\text{ and }\;A_{2}\exp\left[-\frac{n\rho^{2}}{\phi_{s_{\theta}}^{2}\log^{2}\left(\frac{\phi_{s_{\theta}}}{\rho}\right)}-\phi_{1}s_{\theta}\log d\right]\leq\delta.

If
\zeta=A_{3}A_{\varphi}\sqrt{\frac{\phi_{1}\log(d/\delta)}{n}}
, then the rate of consistency
r_{\pi}=r_{\pi}(n,\delta)
in Assumption
5
can be taken as:

r_{\pi}(n,\delta)=\frac{A_{4}L_{\varphi}A_{\varphi}}{\rho}\sqrt{\frac{\phi_{1}\phi_{s_{\theta}}s_{\theta}\log(d/\delta)}{n}\left[\log\left(\frac{n}{\delta}\right)+s_{\theta}\log\left(\frac{ed}{s_{\theta}}\right)\right]}.

The proof of Proposition
C.1
is in
Section E.8
, which relies on the consistency of
\left|\left|\widehat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\right|\right|_{2}
obtained from the Lasso-type generalized linear regression (
27
) as in Lemma
E.2
. It suggests that the rate of convergence for
r_{\pi}
is of the order
O_{P}\left(\frac{s_{\theta}\log d}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
, provided that all other model parameters
L_{\varphi},A_{\varphi},\rho,\phi_{1},\phi_{s_{\theta}}>0
are independent of
d
and
n
with
\delta=\frac{1}{d}
and
d>n
.

Additionally, we conduct comparative experiments under the simpler MCAR setting for completeness and interpretations of Example
1
.

Table 7
:
Simulation results for our proposed debiasing method when the propensity scores are estimated by various machine learning methods under the circulant symmetric covariance matrix
\Sigma^{\mathrm{cs}}
, Gaussian noises
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and the new MAR setting (

). Note that the “Avg-MAE” for propensity score estimation is independent of the choices of
\beta_{0}
and
x

D.2
Simulations Under the MCAR Setting

We follow our comparative simulation designs in
Section 4.1
but define the missingness indicators
R_{i},i=1,...,n
through a MCAR mechanism as
\mathrm{P}(R_{i}=1)=0.7
for
i=1,...,n
. Under the MCAR setting, both the circulant symmetric matrix
\Sigma^{\mathrm{cs}}
and the Toeplitz matrix
\Sigma^{\mathrm{ar}}
will have their inverses satisfying our sufficient conditions for the sparse approximation to the population dual solution
\ell_{0}(x)
in Lemma
B.1
as we have illuminated in Example
1
as well. The simulation results are shown in
Table 8
and
Table 9
under the Gaussian noise
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
setting, and the conclusions are similar to the main takeaways from the MAR simulation studies in
Section 4.2
. We omit the simulation results for other types of the noise distribution, since they share similar patterns with the Gaussian noise setting and we have demonstrated in
Section 4.3
that our proposed debiasing method is robust to other heavy-tailed noise distributions.

Appendix E
Proofs

This section consists of the proofs for theorems and propositions in the main paper as well as other auxiliary results.

E.1
Additional Notations

We denote by
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
a sequence of independent
\mathcal{X}
-valued random elements with common probability measure
\mathrm{P}
. In most cases of this paper,
\mathcal{X}
is the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any measure
Q
and any real-valued
Q
-integrable function
f
on the measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
, we write
Qf=\int fdQ
and denote by
L_{p}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},Q)
with
p\in[1,\infty)
as the space of all real-valued measurable functions
f
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
with finite
L_{p}(Q)
-norm,
i.e.
,
\left|\left|f\right|\right|_{Q,p}:=\left(\int|f|^{p}dQ\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty
. The empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
associated with observations
\{X_{1},...,X_{n}\}
is defined as the random measure on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
given by
\mathbb{P}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
, where
\delta_{x}
is the Dirac measure at point
x\in\mathcal{X}
. We denote the empirical process indexed by a class
\mathcal{F}
by
\mathbb{G}_{n}(f)=\sqrt{n}\left(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathrm{P}\right)f=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f(X_{i})-\mathrm{P}f\right),f\in\mathcal{F}
. For any measure
Q
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
, we set
\left|\left|Q\right|\right|_{\mathcal{F}}=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|Qf|
.

Analogous to the
\psi_{\alpha}
-Orlicz norm (
2
) for random variables, we define the
\psi_{\alpha}
-Orlicz norm for a real-valued
Q
-integrable function
f
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
as:

\left|\left|f\right|\right|_{Q,\psi_{\alpha}}=\inf\left\{t>0:\int\psi_{\alpha}\left(\frac{|f|}{t}\right)dQ\leq 1\right\}

(31)

for
\psi_{\alpha}(u)=\exp(u^{\alpha})-1
.

E.2
Proofs of Propositions
1
and
2

Proposition 1
.

The dual form of the debiasing program (
4
) is given by

\min_{\ell(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{\frac{1}{4n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\pi}_{i}\left[X_{i}^{T}\ell(x)\right]^{2}+x^{T}\ell(x)+\frac{\gamma}{n}\left|\left|\ell(x)\right|\right|_{1}\right\}.

If strong duality holds, then the solution
\widehat{\bm{w}}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the primal debiasing program (
4
) and the solution
\widehat{\ell}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to its dual formulation (
8
) satisfy the following identity as:

\widehat{w}_{i}(x)=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\cdot X_{i}^{T}\widehat{\ell}(x),\quad i=1,...,n.

Proof of Proposition
1
.

The proof is mainly adopted from Lemma 5 of
Giessing and Wang 2023
. Recall from (
4
) that the debiasing program is a convex optimization problem and can be written as the matrix form:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\min_{\bm{w}:=\bm{w}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{1}{2}\bm{w}^{T}\widehat{\Psi}\bm{w}\\
&\text{subject to }\,A\bm{w}\preceq b,\end{split}

(32)

where
\widehat{\Psi}=\mathtt{Diag}\left(2\widehat{\pi}_{1},...,2\widehat{\pi}_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
A=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\begin{pmatrix}-\bm{X}^{T}\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}\\
\bm{X}^{T}\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times n}
with
\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}=\mathtt{Diag}\left(\widehat{\pi}_{1},...,\widehat{\pi}_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
\bm{X}=(X_{1},...,X_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, as well as
b=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\gamma}{n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{d}-x\\
\frac{\gamma}{n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{d}+x\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. This is because the box constraint
\left|\left|x-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\cdot\widehat{\pi}_{i}\cdot X_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\gamma}{n}
in (
4
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle-\frac{\gamma}{n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{d}\leq x-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\cdot\widehat{\pi}_{i}\cdot X_{i}\leq\frac{\gamma}{n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{d}

\displaystyle\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\begin{pmatrix}-\bm{X}^{T}\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}\\
\bm{X}^{T}\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}\end{pmatrix}\bm{w}\leq\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\gamma}{n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{d}-x\\
\frac{\gamma}{n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{d}+x\end{pmatrix},

where
\bm{w}=(w_{1},...,w_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The Lagrange dual function is given by

g(\xi)=\inf_{\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\bm{w}^{T}\widehat{\Psi}\bm{w}+\xi^{T}(A\bm{w}-b)\right]=-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{T}A\widehat{\Psi}^{-1}A^{T}\xi-\xi^{T}b,

(33)

where
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and the infimum is attained at
\bm{w}=-\widehat{\Psi}^{-1}A^{T}\xi
. It implies the dual of (
32
) as:

\displaystyle\max_{\xi:=\xi(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{T}A\widehat{\Psi}^{-1}A^{T}\xi-\xi^{T}b\right]

\displaystyle\text{subject to }\,\xi\succeq\bm{0}.

If strong duality holds, then the solution
\widehat{\bm{w}}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the primal problem can be related to the solution
\widehat{\xi}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
to the dual problem as:

\widehat{\bm{w}}(x)=-\widehat{\Psi}^{-1}A^{T}\widehat{\xi}(x).

(34)

Since
\widehat{\xi}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
is the vector of optimal Lagrangian multipliers associated with the constraints
A\bm{w}\preceq b
in the primal problem (
32
), it satisfies the complementary slackness conditions

\left[\widehat{\xi}(x)\right]_{k}\geq 0\quad\text{ and }\quad\left[A\widehat{\bm{w}}(x)\right]_{k}<b_{k}\quad\implies\quad\left[\widehat{\xi}(x)\right]_{k}=0,

for any
k=1,...,2d
. We write
\widehat{\xi}(x)=\left(\bar{\mu},\underline{\mu}\right)
with
\bar{\mu},\underline{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
. The above complementary slackness conditions indicate that

\bar{\mu}_{k}\cdot\underline{\mu}_{k}=0,\quad k=1,...,d,

(35)

because by design, at least one of the inequalities
\left[A\widehat{\bm{w}}(x)\right]_{k}\leq b_{k}
and
\left[A\widehat{\bm{w}}(x)\right]_{d+k}\leq b_{d+k}
should be strict. Take
\widehat{\ell}(x):=(-\bar{\mu}+\underline{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Equation (
35
) implies that

|\widehat{\ell}_{k}(x)|=\bar{\mu}_{k}+\underline{\mu}_{k},\quad k=1,...,d,

and thus,

-\frac{1}{2}\xi(x)^{T}A\widehat{\Psi}^{-1}A^{T}\xi(x)-\xi(x)^{T}b=-\frac{1}{2n}\widehat{\ell}(x)^{T}\bm{X}^{T}\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}\widehat{\Psi}^{-1}\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}\bm{X}\widehat{\ell}(x)-x^{T}\widehat{\ell}(x)-\frac{\gamma}{n}\left|\left|\widehat{\ell}(x)\right|\right|_{1}.

Since every
\ell(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be written as
\ell(x)=\left(-\bar{\mu}+\underline{\mu}\right)
for some
\bar{\mu},\underline{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, the above equation implies that
\widehat{\ell}(x)
is the solution to the dual problem as:

\displaystyle\min_{\ell(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\frac{1}{2n}\ell(x)^{T}\bm{X}^{T}\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}\widehat{\Psi}^{-1}\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}\bm{X}\ell(x)+x^{T}\ell(x)+\frac{\gamma}{n}\left|\left|\ell(x)\right|\right|_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\equiv\min_{\ell(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{\frac{1}{4n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\pi}_{i}\left[X_{i}^{T}\ell(x)\right]^{2}+x^{T}\ell(x)+\frac{\gamma}{n}\left|\left|\ell(x)\right|\right|_{1}\right\}.

Finally, we observe from (
34
) that
\widehat{\bm{w}}(x)=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\widehat{\Psi}^{-1}\widehat{\bm{\Pi}}\bm{X}\widehat{\ell}(x)
and therefore,

\widehat{w}_{i}(x)=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\cdot X_{i}^{T}\widehat{\ell}(x),\quad i=1,...,n.

The proof is completed.
∎

Proposition 2
.

Under Assumption
1
, the conditional mean squared error of
\sqrt{n}\,m^{\mathrm{debias}}(x;\bm{w})
given
\bm{X}=(X_{1},...,X_{n})^{T}
has the following decomposition as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[\left(\sqrt{n}\,m^{\mathrm{debias}}(x;\bm{w})-\sqrt{n}\,m_{0}(x)\right)^{2}\Big|\bm{X}\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2}\pi(X_{i})}_{\text{Conditional variance I}}+\underbrace{\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)^{T}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2}\pi(X_{i})\left(1-\pi(X_{i})\right)X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\right]\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)}_{\text{Conditional variance II}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}-x\right)^{T}\sqrt{n}\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)\right]^{2}}_{\text{Conditional bias}}.

Proof of Proposition
2
.

Under the MAR condition in Assumption
1
, the conditional mean of (
11
) (scaled by a factor
\sqrt{n}
) given
\bm{X}=(X_{1},...,X_{n})^{T}
can be written as:

\mathrm{E}\left[\sqrt{n}\,m^{\text{debias}}(x;\bm{w})\big|\bm{X}\right]=\sqrt{n}\,x^{T}\beta+\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}^{T}\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right).

Then, we calculate the conditional mean squared error of (
11
) under model (
1
) with
m_{0}(x)=\mathrm{E}(Y|X=x)=x^{T}\beta_{0}
as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[\left(\sqrt{n}\,m^{\text{debias}}(x;\bm{w})-\sqrt{n}\,m_{0}(x)\right)^{2}\Big|\bm{X}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}\left\{\left[\sqrt{n}\,m^{\text{debias}}(x;\bm{w})-\mathrm{E}\left(\sqrt{n}\,m^{\text{debias}}(x;\bm{w})\big|\bm{X}\right)\right]^{2}\Big|\bm{X}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\{\mathrm{E}\left[\sqrt{n}\,m^{\text{debias}}(x;\bm{w})\big|\bm{X}\right]-\sqrt{n}\,m_{0}(x)\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}\left\{\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(R_{i}-\pi(X_{i}))X_{i}^{T}\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}R_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right]^{2}\Bigg|\bm{X}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}-x\right)^{T}\sqrt{n}\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2}\pi(X_{i})\left(1-\pi(X_{i})\right)\left[X_{i}^{T}\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)\right]^{2}+\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2}\pi(X_{i})

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}-x\right)^{T}\sqrt{n}\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2}\pi(X_{i})}_{\text{Conditional variance I}}+\underbrace{\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)^{T}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2}\pi(X_{i})\left(1-\pi(X_{i})\right)X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\right]\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)}_{\text{Conditional variance II}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}-x\right)^{T}\sqrt{n}\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)\right]^{2}}_{\text{Conditional bias}}.

The result thus follows.
∎

Remark E.1
.

Notice that we can upper bound the “Conditional variance II” term as:

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)^{T}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2}\pi(X_{i})\left(1-\pi(X_{i})\right)X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\right]\sqrt{n}\left(\beta_{0}-\beta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sqrt{n}\left|\left|\beta_{0}-\beta\right|\right|_{1}\right)^{2}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left|\left|\sqrt{\pi(X_{i})\left(1-\pi(X_{i})\right)}\cdot w_{i}X_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}.

Thus, for some finite-sample case when the “Conditional variance II” term is not negligible, one may consider optimizing the weights
\bm{w}=(w_{1},...,w_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with a more precise debiasing program as:

\displaystyle\min_{\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\pi}_{i}w_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{ subject to }\left|\left|x-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\widehat{\pi}_{i}X_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}}{n}\quad\text{ and }\quad\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left|\left|\sqrt{\widehat{\pi}_{i}\left(1-\widehat{\pi}_{i}\right)}\cdot w_{i}X_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}}{n},

where
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}>0
are two tuning parameters. However, it will be difficult to derive the dual form of the above debiasing program and tune the two parameters
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
simultaneously. Given that there are no asymptotic efficiency gains for using the above program than our proposed one (
4
) by Lemma
6
, we still recommend our approach even for finite-sample cases.

E.3
Proof of
Theorem 3

Theorem 3

(Consistency of the Lasso pilot estimate with complete-case data)
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
A_{1},A_{2}>0
be some absolute constants. Suppose that Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
(more precisely, Eq.(
17
)) hold and
n
is sufficiently large so that

\sqrt{\frac{s_{\beta}\log(ed/s_{\beta})}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}<\min\left\{\frac{\kappa_{R}^{2}}{A_{1}\phi_{s_{\beta}}},1\right\}.

(a)

If
\lambda>\frac{4}{n}\left|\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}X_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}>0
and
d\geq s_{\beta}+2
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left|\left|\widehat{\beta}-\beta_{0}\right|\right|_{2}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{s_{\beta}}\lambda}{\kappa_{R}^{2}}.

(b)

If, in addition, Assumption
3
holds and
\lambda=A_{2}\sigma_{\epsilon}\sqrt{\frac{\phi_{1}\log(d/\delta)}{n}}
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widehat{\beta}-\beta_{0}\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sigma_{\epsilon}}{\kappa_{R}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\phi_{1}s_{\beta}\log(d/\delta)}{n}}.

Proof of
Theorem 3
.

(a) Since
\widehat{\beta}
minimizes the function
L(\beta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\left(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{T}\beta\right)^{2}+\lambda\left|\left|\beta\right|\right|_{1}
with complete-case data, we know that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\left(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{T}\widehat{\beta}\right)^{2}+\lambda\left|\left|\widehat{\beta}\right|\right|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\left(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}\right)^{2}+\lambda\left|\left|\beta_{0}\right|\right|_{1}.

Then, under the linear model (
1
) in Assumption
1
, it implies by direct calculations or Taylor’s expansion that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\left(X_{i}^{T}\widehat{v}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}R_{i}X_{i}^{T}\widehat{v}+\lambda\left(\left|\left|\beta_{0}\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\beta_{0}+\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{1}\right)

(36)

with
\widehat{v}=\widehat{\beta}-\beta_{0}
. Notice that
\beta_{0,S_{\beta}^{c}}=0
, so we have
\left|\left|\beta_{0}\right|\right|_{1}=\left|\left|\beta_{0,S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}
and

\left|\left|\beta_{0}+\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{1}=\left|\left|\beta_{0,S_{\beta}}+\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}+\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\geq\left|\left|\beta_{0,S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}+\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}.

Plugging this lower bound into (
36
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\left(X_{i}^{T}\widehat{v}\right)^{2}&\leq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}R_{i}X_{i}^{T}\widehat{v}+\lambda\left(\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\right)\\
&\leq\frac{2\left|\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}X_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}}{n}\cdot\left|\left|\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{1}+\lambda\left(\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\right),\end{split}

(37)

where we apply Hölder’s inequality to obtain the second inequality. Under the condition that
\frac{4}{n}\left|\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}X_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}<\lambda
, we can proceed (
37
) as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}0\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\left(X_{i}^{T}\widehat{v}\right)^{2}&\leq\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}+\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\right)+\lambda\left(\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\right)\\
&=\frac{3\lambda}{2}\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}.\end{split}

(38)

Rearranging this inequality shows that
\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\leq 3\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}
. Thus, the error
\widehat{v}=\widehat{\beta}-\beta_{0}
belongs to the cone
\mathcal{C}_{1}(S_{\beta},3)
. By Assumption
4
(more precisely, Eq.(
17
)) and Lemma
E.7
(taking
\vartheta=3
), we know that there exists an absolute constant
A_{0}>0
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\left(X_{i}^{T}\widehat{v}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{E}\left[R_{i}\left(X_{i}^{T}\widehat{v}\right)^{2}\right]-A_{0}\phi_{s_{\beta}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)+s_{\beta}\log(ed/s_{\beta})}{n}}+\frac{\log(1/\delta)+s_{\beta}\log(ed/s_{\beta})}{n}\right]\left|\left|\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left[\kappa_{R}^{2}-A_{0}\phi_{s_{\beta}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)+s_{\beta}\log(ed/s_{\beta})}{n}}+\frac{\log(1/\delta)+s_{\beta}\log(ed/s_{\beta})}{n}\right)\right]\left|\left|\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left[\kappa_{R}^{2}-2A_{0}\phi_{s_{\beta}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)+s_{\beta}\log(ed/s_{\beta})}{n}}\right]\left|\left|\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\kappa_{R}^{2}}{2}\left|\left|\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{2}^{2},

where the last two inequalities hold under our growth condition

\sqrt{\frac{s_{\beta}\log(ed/s_{\beta})}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}<\min\left\{\frac{\kappa_{R}^{2}}{A_{1}\phi_{s_{\beta}}},1\right\}

with
A_{1}=4A_{0}>0
when
n
is sufficiently large. Substituting the above inequality into (
38
) yields that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa_{R}^{2}}{2}\left|\left|\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\lambda}{2}\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}}\right|\right|_{1}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left|\left|\widehat{v}_{S_{\beta}^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda\sqrt{s_{\beta}}\left|\left|\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{2},

where the second inequality is due to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. The result now follows by dividing
\left|\left|\widehat{v}\right|\right|_{2}
on both sides of the inequality.

(b) It suffices to show that
\frac{4}{n}\left|\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}X_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}
is upper bounded by
\sigma_{\epsilon}\sqrt{\frac{\phi_{1}\log(d/\delta)}{n}}
up to some absolute constant with probability at least
1-\delta
. Notice that

\frac{4}{n}\left|\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}X_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}4\epsilon_{i}R_{i}X_{ik}\right|,

where
X_{ik}
is the
k
-th element of the covariate
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
i=1,...,n
. By Assumptions
2
and
3
together with Lemma
E.13
(see also Lemma 2.7.7 in
Vershynin 2018
), we know that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left|\left|4\epsilon_{i}R_{i}X_{ik}\right|\right|_{\psi_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left[4\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left|\left|X_{ik}\right|\right|_{\psi_{2}}\right)\left|\left|R_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right|\right|_{\psi_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\phi_{1}}\cdot\sigma_{\epsilon}

for all
i=1,...,n
. Hence, by the union bound and Bernstein’s inequality (Corollary 2.8.3 in
Vershynin 2018
), we obtain that for all
t>0
, there exists an absolute constant
A_{3}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\left(\frac{4}{n}\left|\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}X_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}>t\right)

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\mathrm{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}4\epsilon_{i}R_{i}X_{ik}\right|>t\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2d\cdot\exp\left(-nA_{3}\min\left\{\frac{t}{\sigma_{\epsilon}\sqrt{\phi_{1}}},\frac{t^{2}}{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\phi_{1}}\right\}\right).

Thus, we conclude that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n}\left|\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}X_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right|\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma_{\epsilon}\sqrt{\phi_{1}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma_{\epsilon}\sqrt{\frac{\phi_{1}\log(d/\delta)}{n}},

where we notice that
\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}}
dominates the bound under our growth condition
\sqrt{\frac{s_{\beta}\log(ed/s_{\beta})}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}<1
. Finally, the bound on
\left|\left|\widehat{\beta}-\beta_{0}\right|\right|_{2}
follows from substituting
\lambda=A_{2}\sigma_{\epsilon}\sqrt{\frac{\phi_{1}\log(d/\delta)}{n}}
for some absolute constant into the upper bound of
\left|\left|\widehat{\beta}-\beta_{0}\right|\right|_{2}
derived in (a).
∎

E.4
Proof of
Theorem 4

Before presenting the proof of
Theorem 4
, we first establish the consistency of
\widehat{\ell}(x)
to the sparse
r_{\ell}
-approximation
\widetilde{\ell}(x)
of the exact population dual solution (
19
) under Assumption
6
and the known/oracle propensity scores
\pi_{i}=\pi(X_{i}),i=1,...,n
in the following lemma.

Lemma E.1

(Consistency of the dual solution
\widehat{\ell}(x)
when the propensity score is known)
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a fixed covariate vector, and
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}>0
be some large absolute constants. Suppose that Assumptions
1
,
2
,
4
, and
6
hold as well as

\sqrt{\frac{s_{\ell}(x)\log(ed/s_{\ell}(x))}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}<\min\left\{\frac{\kappa_{R}^{2}}{A_{1}\phi_{s_{\ell}(x)}},1\right\}\quad\text{ and }\quad d\geq s_{\ell}(x)+2.

If
\gamma=A_{2}\frac{\sqrt{\phi_{1}}}{\kappa_{R}}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}\sqrt{n\log(d/\delta)}
and
r_{\ell}<\left(\frac{A_{2}^{2}\,\kappa_{R}^{6}\,\phi_{1}}{A_{3}^{2}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}\phi_{s_{\ell}(x)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widehat{\ell}(x)-\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}}{\kappa_{R}^{2}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\phi_{1}}{\kappa_{R}^{2}}}\sqrt{\frac{s_{\ell}(x)\log(d/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{\phi_{s_{\ell}(x)}}{\kappa_{R}^{2}}\cdot r_{\ell}\right].

Proof of Lemma
E.1
.

Since
\widehat{\ell}(x)
is the solution to the dual program (
8
), we know that

\frac{1}{4n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(X_{i})\left[X_{i}^{T}\widehat{\ell}(x)\right]^{2}+x^{T}\widehat{\ell}(x)+\frac{\gamma}{n}\left|\left|\widehat{\ell}(x)\right|\right|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(X_{i})\left[X_{i}^{T}\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right]^{2}+x^{T}\widetilde{\ell}(x)+\frac{\gamma}{n}\left|\left|\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right|\right|_{1}.

Some rearrangements (or using Taylor’s expansion) give us that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left[\widehat{\ell}(x)-\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right]^{T}\left[\frac{1}{4n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\right]\left[\widehat{\ell}(x)-\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right]\\
&\quad+\left[\widehat{\ell}(x)-\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right]^{T}\left[\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\right]\left[\widetilde{\ell}(x)-\ell_{0}(x)\right]\\
&\leq\left[\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\ell_{0}(x)+x\right]^{T}\left[\widetilde{\ell}(x)-\widehat{\ell}(x)\right]+\frac{\gamma}{n}\left[\left|\left|\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\widehat{\ell}(x)\right|\right|_{1}\right].\end{split}

(39)

In the sequel, we write
\widehat{\Delta}(x)=\widehat{\ell}(x)-\widetilde{\ell}(x)
. By Hölder’s inequality and the (reverse) triangle’s inequality together with the sparsity of
\widetilde{\ell}(x)
under Assumption
6
, the right-hand side of (
39
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left[\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\ell_{0}(x)+x\right]^{T}\left[\widetilde{\ell}(x)-\widehat{\ell}(x)\right]+\frac{\gamma}{n}\left[\left|\left|\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\widehat{\ell}(x)\right|\right|_{1}\right]\\
&\leq\left|\left|\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\ell_{0}(x)+x\right|\right|_{\infty}\left|\left|\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right|\right|_{1}\\
&\quad+\frac{\gamma}{n}\left[\left|\left|\left[\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)}\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\left[\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)}+\left[\widetilde{\ell}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)}\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\left[\widehat{\ell}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\right]\\
&\leq\frac{\gamma}{5n}\left[\left|\left|\left[\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)}\right|\right|_{1}+\left|\left|\left[\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\right]+\frac{\gamma}{n}\left[\left|\left|\left[\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)}\right|\right|_{1}-\left|\left|\left[\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\right]\\
&=\frac{6\gamma}{5n}\left|\left|\left[\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)}\right|\right|_{1}-\frac{4\gamma}{5n}\left|\left|\left[\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)^{c}}\right|\right|_{1}\\
&\leq\frac{6\gamma}{5n}\sqrt{s_{\ell}(x)}\left|\left|\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right|\right|_{2},\end{split}

(40)

where
\left|\left|\left[\widehat{\Delta}(x)\right]_{S_{\ell}(x)}\right|\right|_{1}=\sum_{k\in S_{\ell}(x)}\left|\widehat{\Delta}_{k}(x)\right|
.

Next, we know from Lemma
E.9
with
a_{0}=5
that

\widehat{\ell}(x)-\widetilde{\ell}(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(S_{\ell}(x),3\right)\bigcup B_{d}^{(1)}\left(0,\,\frac{13r_{\ell}^{2}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}^{2}}{2\kappa_{R}^{4}}\cdot\frac{\nu}{\gamma}\right)\equiv\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(S_{\ell}(x),3\right)\bigcup B_{d}^{(1)}(0,r_{1}),

where
B_{d}^{(1)}(0,r)=\left\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left|\left|v\right|\right|_{1}\leq r\right\}
and
r_{1}\equiv\frac{13r_{\ell}^{2}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}^{2}}{2\kappa_{R}^{4}}\cdot\frac{\nu}{\gamma}
.

Now, by Lemma
E.12
, we know that there exist absolute constants
A_{2},A_{2}^{\prime}>0
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\left|\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(X_{i})X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\ell_{0}(x)+2x\right|\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq A_{2}^{\prime}\frac{\sqrt{\phi_{1}}}{\kappa_{R}}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq A_{2}\frac{\sqrt{\phi_{1}}}{\kappa_{R}}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\equiv\frac{\gamma}{n},

where we note that
\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}}
asymptotically dominates the bound under our growth condition
\sqrt{\frac{s_{\ell}(x)\log(ed/s_{\ell}(x))}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}<1
. Similarly, we obtain from Lemma
E.11
that there exists an absolute constant
A_{3}>0
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in\mathcal{C}_{2}\left(S_{\ell}(x),\vartheta_{\ell}\right)\cap B_{d}(0,1)}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i}\left[X_{i}^{T}v\right]^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2A_{3}}{13}\cdot\phi_{s_{\ell}(x)}\left[1+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)+s_{\ell}(x)\log\left(d/s_{\ell}(x)\right)}{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2A_{3}}{13}\cdot\phi_{s_{\ell}(x)}\equiv\frac{\nu}{n}.

Hence, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\frac{\kappa_{R}^{2}}{\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}{13\nu}}\geq\left(\frac{A_{2}^{2}\,\kappa_{R}^{6}\,\phi_{1}}{A_{3}^{2}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}\phi_{s_{\ell}(x)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}>r_{\ell},

where the last inequality follows from our condition. Hence, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have that
r_{1}=\frac{13r_{\ell}^{2}\left|\left|x\right|\right|_{2}^{2}}{2\kappa_{R}^{4}}\cdot\frac{\nu}{\gamma}<1
, and

\left[\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(S_{\ell}(x),3\right)\bigcup B_{d}^{(1)}(0,r_{1})\right]\bigcap\mathbb{S}^{d-1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(S_{\ell}(x),3\right)\bigcap\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

Therefore, by Assumption
4
together with Lemma
E.8
